filters used in [section 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3 "3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), namely *K-hop Locality* ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition1 "Proposition 1. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")) and a *non-increasing Dirichlet Energy* for
diffusion-like multipliers ([2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")). Throughout,$L$ denotes a symmetric positive semidefinite (vertex) sheaf Laplacian on a
finite graph $G=(V,E)$, with the usual block sparsity pattern induced by
edges, and $p,q$ denote real polynomials.

##### K-hop Locality.

In order to prove K-hop locality principle, we first make precise how the sparsity pattern of $L^{k}$ reflects walks in $G$:

###### Lemma 4 (Support of Powers and Walks).

Let $L$ be a block matrix indexed by $V$ such that its off-diagonal block$(v,u)$ is nonzero only when $(v,u)\in E$, and diagonal blocks $(v,v)$ are
arbitrary. Then for any $k\geq 1$ and any pair $(v,u)\in V\times V$: $\big(L^{k}\big)_{vu}\neq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\text{ a walk }v=v_{0}\sim v_{1}\sim\cdots\sim v_{\ell}=u\text{ in }G\text{ of length }\ell\leq k$. In particular, if $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>k$ (no walk of length $\leq k$), then$\big(L^{k}\big)_{vu}=0$.

###### Proof.

We proceed by induction on $k$.

*Base case $k=1$.* By assumption on $L$, for $v\neq u$ we have $L_{vu}\neq 0$ only if $(v,u)\in E$,
which is a walk of length $1$. For $v=u$ the block $L_{vv}$ may be nonzero,
corresponding to the trivial walk of length $0\leq 1$. Hence the claim holds.

*Induction step.* Assume the statement holds for some $k\geq 1$. Consider $L^{k+1}=L^{k}L$. Its$(v,u)$ block satisfies: $\big(L^{k+1}\big)_{vu}=\sum_{w\in V}\big(L^{k}\big)_{vw}\,L_{wu}$. If $\big(L^{k+1}\big)_{vu}\neq 0$, then there exists some $w$ such that$\big(L^{k}\big)_{vw}\neq 0$ and $L_{wu}\neq 0$. By the induction hypothesis,$\big(L^{k}\big)_{vw}\neq 0$ implies the existence of a walk$v=v_{0}\sim v_{1}\sim\cdots\sim v_{\ell}=w$ of length $\ell\leq k$. The condition$L_{wu}\neq 0$ means either $w=u$ (diagonal block) or $(w,u)\in E$ (off-diagonal
edge block). In particular:

- •

  If $w=u$, we obtain a walk from $v$ to $u$ of length $\ell\leq k<k+1$ by appending a trivial step at $u$.

- •

  If $(w,u)\in E$, we can append this edge to the walk $v\leadsto w$ and obtain a walk $v=v_{0}\sim\cdots\sim v_{\ell}=w\sim u$ of length $\ell+1\leq k+1$.

In both cases, there exists a walk from $v$ to $u$ of length at most $k+1$. This
proves the implication in the lemma for $k+1$, closing the induction. For the final statement, if $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>k$ then by definition there is
no walk from $v$ to $u$ of length $\leq k$, so the implication above forces$\big(L^{k}\big)_{vu}=0$.
∎

We can now prove the *K-hop locality of Polynomial Filters*.

###### Proof of [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition1 "Proposition 1. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

Recall that, as defined in [Equation 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Definition 1 (Polynomial learnable sheaf spectral filter). ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), we have: $p(L)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}c_{k}L^{k}$, and more specifically, for $v,u\in V$ we have $\big(p(L)\big)_{vu}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}c_{k}\,\big(L^{k}\big)_{vu}$. If $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>K$, then $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>k$ for all $0\leq k\leq K$. For $k=0$ we have $L^{0}=I$, so $(L^{0})_{vu}=0$ whenever $v\neq u$. For $k\geq 1$, [Lemma 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmlemma4 "Lemma 4 (Support of Powers and Walks). ‣ K-hop Locality. ‣ A.5 Polynomial Filters: K-hop Locality and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") implies that $\big(L^{k}\big)_{vu}=0$ whenever $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>k$. Therefore, each term in the sum vanishes, and we obtain $\big(p(L)\big)_{vu}=0$ whenever $\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,u)>K$, as claimed.
∎

##### Commutation and Dirichlet Energy Monotonicity.

We now justify [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

###### Proof of [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

Let $L$ be symmetric PSD and let $L=U\Lambda U^{\top}$ be its spectral
decomposition, with $\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{nd})$ and$U$ orthogonal. For any real polynomial $r$ we have, by [Lemma 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmlemma3 "Lemma 3 (Polynomial Functional Calculus). ‣ A.3 Spectral Multipliers and Functional Calculus ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), we have $r(L)=U\,r(\Lambda)\,U^{\top}$, where $r(\Lambda)$ is diagonal with entries $r(\lambda_{i})$.

*Commutation and Polynomial Composition.* Let $p,q$ be real polynomials. Then:

|  | $p(L)q(L)=\big(Up(\Lambda)U^{\top}\big)\big(Uq(\Lambda)U^{\top}\big)=U\,p(\Lambda)\,(U^{\top}U)\,q(\Lambda)\,U^{\top}=U\,p(\Lambda)q(\Lambda)\,U^{\top}$ |  | (35) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Since $p(\Lambda)$ and $q(\Lambda)$ are diagonal matrices, they commute and
their product is diagonal with entries $p(\lambda_{i})q(\lambda_{i})$. In other
words, we have: $p(\Lambda)q(\Lambda)=q(\Lambda)p(\Lambda)=(pq)(\Lambda)$ and therefore, $p(L)q(L)=U\,(pq)(\Lambda)\,U^{\top}=(pq)(L)$. By symmetry of the argument, $q(L)p(L)=(pq)(L)$ as well. This proves the commutation and composition identity.

*Dirichlet Energy under a Diffusion Multiplier.* Let $x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})$ and write $x=U\hat{x}$, so that$\hat{x}=U^{\top}x$ are the sheaf Fourier coefficients. Using the spectral
functional calculus, we have $p(L)x=Up(\Lambda)U^{\top}x=Up(\Lambda)\hat{x}$. The Dirichlet energy of $p(L)x$ with respect to $L$ is:

|  | $\displaystyle\big\langle p(L)x,\ L\,p(L)x\big\rangle=\big\langle Up(\Lambda)\hat{x},\ U\Lambda p(\Lambda)\hat{x}\big\rangle=\hat{x}^{\top}p(\Lambda)^{\top}\Lambda p(\Lambda)\,\hat{x}$ |  | (36) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

where we used orthogonality of $U$ and symmetry of $L$. Since $p(\Lambda)$ and$\Lambda$ are diagonal and real, $p(\Lambda)^{\top}=p(\Lambda)$ and these matrices
all commute. Writing $\mu_{i}:=p(\lambda_{i})$, we obtain, $p(\Lambda)\Lambda p(\Lambda)=\mathrm{diag}\!\big(\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}^{2},\dots,\lambda_{nd}\mu_{nd}^{2}\big)$. We therefore have then:

|  | $\big\langle p(L)x,\ L\,p(L)x\big\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{nd}\lambda_{i}\,\mu_{i}^{2}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{nd}\lambda_{i}\,p(\lambda_{i})^{2}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}$ |  | (37) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

By contrast, the original Dirichlet energy of $x$ is, by [Proposition 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition4 "Proposition 4 (Dirichlet Energy in the Spectral Domain). ‣ A.2 Sheaf Fourier Transform, Plancherel Identity and Dirichlet Energy in the Spectral Domain ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), defined as: $\langle x,Lx\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{nd}\lambda_{i}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}$.
Assume now that $0\leq p(\lambda)\leq 1$ on $\sigma(L)$. Then for every
eigenvalue $\lambda_{i}$ we have $0\leq p(\lambda_{i})\leq 1$ and therefore$0\leq p(\lambda_{i})^{2}\leq 1$. Since each term in the sums is nonnegative
($\lambda_{i}\geq 0$ and $\hat{x}_{i}^{2}\geq 0$), we obtain $\lambda_{i}\,p(\lambda_{i})^{2}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}\;\leq\;\lambda_{i}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}\quad\text{for all }i$. Summing over $i$ yields:

|  | $\big\langle p(L)x,\ L\,p(L)x\big\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{nd}\lambda_{i}\,p(\lambda_{i})^{2}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}\;\leq\;\sum_{i=1}^{nd}\lambda_{i}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}=\langle x,Lx\rangle$ |  | (38) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

which is precisely ([5](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")). This shows that any
spectral multiplier with $0\leq p(\lambda)\leq 1$ on the spectrum acts as a
diffusion-like contraction of Dirichlet energy.
∎

Together, [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition1 "Proposition 1. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") formalise the two key structural properties of PolyNSD layers: *locality* (a single layer implements exactly $K$-hop mixing) and *stability* (diffusion-like multipliers cannot increase disagreement energy).

### A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity

In this subsection we make precise the claims stated in [section 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3 "3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). In particular, we justify why the affine rescaling in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") is a *structural* requirement for
Chebyshev parameterisations, and prove that, once the spectrum of $L$ is mapped
to $[-1,1]$, convex mixtures of first-kind Chebyshev polynomials define
nonexpansive spectral multipliers $p(\widetilde{L})$ with non-increasing
Dirichlet energy. This provides the technical backbone for the design choices
in the Chebyshev-PolyNSD layer used in the main text.

##### Spectral Rescaling to $[-1,1]$.

The affine rescaling that occurs inside the *Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion Layer*, as described in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), is what makes the PolyNSD layer both *numerically stable* and *theoretically controlled*. In particular:

1. 1\.

   *Extremal Property and Uniform Bound.* First-kind Chebyshev polynomials satisfy $T_{k}(\xi)=\cos(k\arccos\xi),&#124;\xi&#124;\leq 1$, and hence:

    |  | $&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;\;\leq\;1\quad\text{for all}\quad\xi\in[-1,1],\ k\geq 0$ |  | (39) |
    | --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

   This extremal/minimax property *only* holds on $[-1,1]$. For $&#124;\xi&#124;>1$,
   Chebyshev polynomials grow exponentially:

    |  | $T_{k}(\xi)=\cosh\!\big(k\,\mathrm{arcosh}&#124;\xi&#124;\big),\qquad&#124;\xi&#124;>1$ |  | (40) |
    | --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

   so $&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;$ behaves like $c(\xi)\,\rho(\xi)^{k}$ with $\rho(\xi)>1$. If we
   were to apply $T_{k}(L)$ directly to a Laplacian whose eigenvalues satisfy$\lambda_{i}>1$, high-degree terms would produce operator norms that grow
   exponentially in $k$. In other words, the very same sequence that is
   beautifully bounded on $[-1,1]$ is catastrophically unbounded as soon as
   eigenvalues leave that interval. By forcing the spectrum of the *rescaled* operator $\widetilde{L}$ to lie in $[-1,1]$, we ensure that every eigenvalue $\tilde{\lambda}_{i}$ sits exactly in the regime where Chebyshev polynomials are uniformly bounded by $1$. This is what makes convex mixtures $\sum_{k}\theta_{k}T_{k}(\widetilde{L})$ nonexpansive
   in operator norm.

2. 2\.

   *Stability of the Recurrence.* The matrix recurrence:

    |  | $T_{0}=x,\qquad T_{1}=\widetilde{L}x,\qquad T_{k+1}=2\widetilde{L}T_{k}-T_{k-1}$ |  | (41) |
    | --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

   is a direct lifting of the scalar recurrence $T_{k+1}(\xi)=2\xi T_{k}(\xi)-T_{k-1}(\xi)$. When $&#124;\xi&#124;\leq 1$, this recurrence is numerically stable: rounding errors do not get amplified exponentially and $T_{k}(\xi)$ remains $\mathcal{O}(1)$ in $k$. If instead some eigenvalues $\lambda_{i}$ satisfy $&#124;\lambda_{i}&#124;>1$, the same recurrence *amplifies* errors and the computed $T_{k}(\lambda_{i})$ will quickly diverge in magnitude,
   even for moderate degrees $K$. Rescaling to $[-1,1]$ by design avoids this
   pathological regime and allows us to evaluate $p(\widetilde{L})$ reliably with$K$ sparse matrix–vector multiplies.

3. 3\.

   *Transferring Scalar Approximation Bounds to Operators.* All classical Chebyshev approximation results, are stated for functions on $[-1,1]$.
   What we want in PolyNSD is an *operator* approximation of a spectral
   multiplier $f(L)$, with $\sigma(L)\subset[0,\lambda_{\max}]$. The affine map $\xi(\lambda)=\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}\lambda-1$ identifies $[0,\lambda_{\max}]$ with $[-1,1]$. We approximate $\tilde{f}(\xi):=f(\tfrac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}(\xi+1))$ by a Chebyshev polynomial$p^{(K)}(\xi)$ on $[-1,1]$, and then lift the scalar bound $\sup_{\xi\in[-1,1]}&#124;\tilde{f}(\xi)-p^{(K)}(\xi)&#124;$ to an operator bound:$\&#124;f(L)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})\&#124;_{2}=\max_{\lambda_{i}\in\sigma(L)}\big&#124;f(\lambda_{i})-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))\big&#124;$.

We now specialise the general energy argument of [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") to the Chebyshev parametrisation used in PolyNSD. This is then easily provable to be the same also for different basis parameterisations. The key point is that convex mixtures of first-kind Chebyshev polynomials on $[-1,1]$ are uniformly bounded by $1$, which immediately implies nonexpansiveness and non-increasing Dirichlet energy after spectral rescaling.

###### Proposition 6 (Boundedness and Energy Monotonicity of Convex Chebyshev Mixtures).

If $\sigma(\widetilde{L})\subset[-1,1]$ and $\theta\in\Delta^{K}$, then $\big\&#124;p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})\big\&#124;_{2}\;\leq\;1$, and for any symmetric PSD $L$ with $\widetilde{L}$ as above and any $x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})$, we have: $\big\langle p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x,\ L\,p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x\big\rangle\;\leq\;\langle x,Lx\rangle$.

###### Proof.

By the spectral theorem, there exists an orthogonal $U$ and diagonal$\widetilde{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})$ with$\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\in[-1,1]$ such that $\widetilde{L}=U\widetilde{\Lambda}U^{\top}$.
By polynomial functional calculus,$p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})=U\,p_{\theta}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\,U^{\top}$ with$p_{\theta}(\widetilde{\Lambda})=\mathrm{diag}(p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}))$.
Since $&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;\leq 1$ for all $\xi\in[-1,1]$ and $\theta\in\Delta^{K}$,
for any $\tilde{\lambda}\in[-1,1]$ we have:

|  | $\bigl&#124;p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda})\bigr&#124;=\Bigl&#124;\sum_{k}\theta_{k}T_{k}(\tilde{\lambda})\Bigr&#124;\leq\sum_{k}\theta_{k}&#124;T_{k}(\tilde{\lambda})&#124;\leq\sum_{k}\theta_{k}=1$ |  | (42) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Thus $&#124;p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})&#124;\leq 1$ for all $i$, and the spectral norm of$p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})$ is $\big\&#124;p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})\big\&#124;_{2}=\big\&#124;p_{\theta}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\big\&#124;_{2}=\max_{i}&#124;p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})&#124;\leq 1$ which proves ([6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition6 "Proposition 6 (Boundedness and Energy Monotonicity of Convex Chebyshev Mixtures). ‣ Spectral Rescaling to [-1,1]. ‣ A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")). For the energy claim, write $L=U\Lambda U^{\top}$ and $x=U\hat{x}$ as in [Proposition 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition4 "Proposition 4 (Dirichlet Energy in the Spectral Domain). ‣ A.2 Sheaf Fourier Transform, Plancherel Identity and Dirichlet Energy in the Spectral Domain ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). Since $\widetilde{L}$ is an affine function of $L$, the two are simultaneously diagonalisable by $U$, and by [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") we obtain $\big\langle p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x,\ L\,p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x\big\rangle=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\,p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})^{2}\,\hat{x}_{i}^{2}$. We have $0\leq p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})^{2}\leq 1$ for all $i$ by the previous bound, so each term satisfies $\lambda_{i}p_{\theta}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})^{2}\hat{x}_{i}^{2}\leq\lambda_{i}\hat{x}_{i}^{2}$. Since all quantities are nonnegative, summing over $i$ yields, as claimed:

|  | $\big\langle p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x,\ L\,p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x\big\rangle\leq\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\hat{x}_{i}^{2}=\langle x,Lx\rangle$ |  | (43) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

∎

So, Proposition [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition6 "Proposition 6 (Boundedness and Energy Monotonicity of Convex Chebyshev Mixtures). ‣ Spectral Rescaling to [-1,1]. ‣ A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") shows that, once the spectrum is
rescaled to $[-1,1]$, the Chebyshev-PolyNSD layer inherits a *hard* $L^{2}$-stability guarantee: the linear core has operator norm at most $1$ and
cannot increase the sheaf Dirichlet energy. This means that
PolyNSD behaves as a spectral regulariser whose frequency response is learned
within a nonexpansive envelope. It is worth noting that the proof strategy is not specific to Chebyshev polynomials: any orthogonal basis $\{B_{k}\}$ that is uniformly
bounded on $[-1,1]$, combined with a convex parametrisation of the coefficients,
admits an analogous argument. Chebyshev polynomials are simply the canonical
instance that simultaneously provide (i) this stability, (ii) near-minimax
approximation properties, and (iii) an efficient three-term recurrence.

## Appendix B PolyNSD Layer

This section makes precise the layer-level construction sketched in [Figure 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S2.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 2 Background and Related Works ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), and provides detailed operator-theoretic
and approximation-theoretic guarantees. We first introduce the Chebyshev-PolyNSD
block, then describe the full architecture, discuss approximation of diffusion
kernels, justify our choices for estimating the spectral scale, and finally
analyse the high-pass skip and residual gating.

### B.1 Chebyshev-PolyNSD Layer

Let $L$ be either the unnormalised sheaf
Laplacian $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ or its degree-normalised variant$\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}$, and let $\lambda_{\max}$ be an upper bound on$\sigma(L)$ (equal to 2 in the normalised case, set analytically or estimated by power iteration in the unnormalised case (see [subsection B.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS4 "B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"))). We first rescale to $[-1,1]$ as in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), i.e., $\widetilde{L}\;=\;\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}L-I\sigma(\widetilde{L})\subset[-1,1]$. *Chebyshev Polynomials* of the first kind satisfy the following:

|  | $T_{0}(\xi)=1,\qquad T_{1}(\xi)=\xi,\qquad T_{k+1}(\xi)=2\xi T_{k}(\xi)-T_{k-1}(\xi)$ |  | (44) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

which we lift to operators via the three-term recurrence:

|  | $T_{0}=x,\qquad T_{1}=\widetilde{L}\,x,\qquad T_{k+1}=2\,\widetilde{L}\,T_{k}-T_{k-1},\quad k\geq 1$ |  | (45) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

A degree-$K$ Chebyshev-PolyNSD filter with trainable logits$\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}$ and $\theta=\mathrm{softmax}(\eta)\in\Delta^{K}$ is
then:

|  | $p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})\,x\;=\;\sum_{k=0}^{K}\theta_{k}\,T_{k}$ |  | (46) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

By construction $&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;\leq 1$ for all $\xi\in[-1,1]$ and$\theta$ is a convex combination, so $&#124;p_{\theta}(\xi)&#124;\leq 1$ on $[-1,1]$.
Therefore $\&#124;p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})\&#124;_{2}\leq 1$, and by [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and [Proposition 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition6 "Proposition 6 (Boundedness and Energy Monotonicity of Convex Chebyshev Mixtures). ‣ Spectral Rescaling to [-1,1]. ‣ A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") Chebyshev-PolyNSD layers are linear *nonexpansive* maps that cannot increase the Dirichlet energy$\langle x,Lx\rangle$.

### B.2 Full Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion Architecture

[Figure 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S2.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 2 Background and Related Works ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")provides a detailed view of the full
PolyNSD architecture, from raw node features to task outputs. We summarise each step, but in subsequent subsections, you can find extensive details for each of them.

*(1) Feature Lifting to Stalks.* Given a graph $G=(V,E)$ with raw node features$x_{v}^{\mathrm{raw}}\in\mathbb{R}^{F_{\mathrm{in}}}$, an input MLP$\phi:\mathbb{R}^{F_{\mathrm{in}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$ produces stalk features$x_{v}=\phi(x_{v}^{\mathrm{raw}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$. Stacking across nodes yields a$0$-cochain $x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})\cong\mathbb{R}^{Nd\times C}$, where$C$ denotes the number of feature channels.

*(2) Sheaf Learner and Restriction Maps.* A sheaf learner $\Psi$ takes as input local edge neighbourhoods (and optionally
edge attributes) and outputs per-incidence restriction maps$\mathcal{F}_{v\unlhd e}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$. We support three families: *diagonal* maps $\mathcal{F}_{v\unlhd e}=\mathrm{diag}(t_{v\unlhd e})$ with$O(&#124;E&#124;d)$ parameters, *bundle/orthogonal* maps$\mathcal{F}_{v\unlhd e}\in O(d)$ acting as parallel transports, and *general* linear maps $\mathcal{F}_{v\unlhd e}\in\mathrm{GL}(d)$ with
maximal expressivity. Optional scalar edge weights $w_{e}$ further modulate the
assembled operator.

*(3) Laplacian Assembly and Spectral Scale.* From $\{\mathcal{F}_{v\unlhd e}\}$ we assemble the vertex sheaf Laplacian$L_{\mathcal{F}}=\delta_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}\delta_{\mathcal{F}}$ (and, optionally,
its degree-normalised variant $\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}$). For the normalised Laplacian we use the
standard spectral bound $\lambda_{\max}=2$, while for the unnormalised case we
either use a Gershgorin-type analytic bound or a short power iteration, as
detailed in [subsection B.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS4 "B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

*(4) PolyNSD + High-pass Skip and Residual Gate.* Given $L$ and $\lambda_{\max}$, we form $\widetilde{L}$ and evaluate a
degree-$K$ Chebyshev-PolyNSD filter $p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})$ via the recurrence [Equation 45](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E45 "Equation 45 ‣ B.1 Chebyshev-PolyNSD Layer ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). To compensate for the intrinsic
low-pass bias of diffusion we add the high-pass skip$h_{\mathrm{hp}}=x-\lambda_{\max}^{-1}Lx$, scaled by a learned scalar$\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}$, and define the pre-nonlinearity$z=p_{\theta}(\widetilde{L})x+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}h_{\mathrm{hp}}$.
A 1-Lipschitz nonlinearity $\phi$ and a diagonal residual gate $\varepsilon$ then produce the update $x^{+}=(I+\tanh\varepsilon)x-\phi(z)$. Its spectral form and global Lipschitz bound are analysed in [subsection B.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS5 "B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

*(5) Readout and depth.* A linear readout head maps the final stalk features to logits or regression
targets. Multiple PolyNSD blocks can be stacked. In practice, we recompute
restriction maps at each depth, but keep the within-block recurrence cheap by
reusing the same sparse Laplacian and storing only two work buffers$(T_{k-1},T_{k})$, so the extra memory overhead is $O(NdC)$ and independent of$K$.

### B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels

This subsection formalises the intuition that Chebyshev-PolyNSD can approximate
diffusion semigroups and other smooth spectral responses with exponentially
small error in the polynomial degree $K$, provided the target response admits
an analytic extension to a neighbourhood of the spectrum. Let $f:[0,\lambda_{\max}]\to\mathbb{R}$ be a continuous spectral response and
consider its affine rescaling to $[-1,1]$ via $\xi(\lambda)=\tfrac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}\lambda-1$. We recall the standard notion of a Bernstein ellipse $E_{\rho}$ with foci at $\pm 1$ and parameter $\rho>1$, and use it to quantify the analyticity region of $f$ (see [Figure 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")).

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3/2512.00242v3/img/2_bernstein_ellipse.png)

*Figure 2: Bernstein ellipses $E_{\rho}$ for increasing $\rho$. Each ellipse has foci at $\pm 1$. The rescaled response$\tilde{f}$ on $E_{\rho}$ implies an exponential-in-$K$ Chebyshev approximation
rate with factor $\rho^{-K}$.*

Our first step is to control the Chebyshev coefficients of a function that is
analytic and bounded on a Bernstein ellipse.

###### Lemma 5 (Chebyshev Coefficients under Bernstein Analyticity).

Let $\tilde{f}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}$ be continuous, and assume that $\tilde{f}$ admits an analytic continuation to the interior of $E_{\rho}$ and is bounded by$M$ there, i.e., $&#124;\tilde{f}(z)&#124;\leq M\quad\text{for all }z\in E_{\rho}$. Let $\tilde{f}(\xi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}T_{k}(\xi),\xi\in[-1,1]$ be its (pointwise convergent) Chebyshev series expansion on $[-1,1]$, where $T_{k}$ are the Chebyshev polynomials of the first kind. Then, for all $k\geq 1$, we have:

|  | $&#124;a_{k}&#124;\;\leq\;\frac{2M}{\rho^{k}}.$ |  | (47) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

###### Proof.

We proceed in three steps: (i) relate $\tilde{f}$ on $[-1,1]$ to a function on a
circle in the complex plane, (ii) express the Chebyshev coefficients $a_{k}$ as
contour integrals, and (iii) bound those integrals by Cauchy’s estimate.

*Step 1: Mapping the Circle to the Ellipse.* Recall that the Bernstein ellipse $E_{\rho}$ is the image of the circle$\{w\in\mathbb{C}:&#124;w&#124;=\rho\}$ under the Joukowski map:$\Phi(w)\;:=\;\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(w+\frac{1}{w}\Bigr)$. More precisely, for $w=\rho e^{i\theta}$ we have $\Phi(w)\in E_{\rho}$, and as $\theta$ ranges over $[0,2\pi)$, $\Phi(w)$ traces the ellipse $E_{\rho}$ once. For $&#124;w&#124;=1$ the same map $\Phi$ parametrises the interval $[-1,1]$ via $\Phi(e^{i\theta})=\cos\theta$.

Define the function $g$ on the annulus $\{w\in\mathbb{C}:\rho^{-1}<&#124;w&#124;<\rho\}$ by $g(w):=\tilde{f}(\Phi(w))=\tilde{f}\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(w+w^{-1}\bigr)\Bigr)$. By assumption, $\tilde{f}$ is analytic on the interior of $E_{\rho}$, and the Joukowski map is analytic and maps $\{w:&#124;w&#124;\leq\rho\}$ onto the closed ellipse and its interior. Hence $g$ is analytic for $&#124;w&#124;<\rho$ (away from $w=0$ the inverse map is well defined, and at $w=0$ analyticity follows by removable singularity, since $\Phi$ is bounded near $0$ and $\tilde{f}$ is analytic in a
neighbourhood of $\Phi(0)$). On the circle $&#124;w&#124;=\rho$ we have $&#124;\Phi(w)&#124;\in E_{\rho}$ so $&#124;\tilde{f}(\Phi(w))&#124;\leq M$, thus:

|  | $&#124;g(w)&#124;\leq M\quad\text{for all }&#124;w&#124;=\rho$ |  | (48) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

∎

###### Proof.

*Step 2: Chebyshev Expansion and Fourier Series of $g$.* On the unit circle $&#124;w&#124;=1$, we can write $w=e^{i\theta}$ and$\Phi(w)=\cos\theta$. The Chebyshev polynomials satisfy $T_{k}(\cos\theta)=\cos(k\theta)\text{for all }k\geq 0$, so the Chebyshev series expansion of $\tilde{f}$ on $[-1,1]$ becomes a cosine series in the angular variable:

|  | $\tilde{f}(\cos\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}T_{k}(\cos\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}\cos(k\theta),\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}$ |  | (49) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

On the other hand, using $w=e^{i\theta}$, we have $g(e^{i\theta})=\tilde{f}\bigl(\Phi(e^{i\theta})\bigr)=\tilde{f}(\cos\theta)$, so, $g$ is restricted to the unit circle has the same real cosine expansion. We now express this cosine expansion in terms of the complex Fourier coefficients of $g$. Recall that $\cos(k\theta)=\tfrac{1}{2}(e^{ik\theta}+e^{-ik\theta})$, so [Equation 49](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E49 "Equation 49 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") becomes:

|  | $g(e^{i\theta})=\tilde{f}(\cos\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}\cos(k\theta)=\frac{a_{0}}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{k}}{2}\bigl(e^{ik\theta}+e^{-ik\theta}\bigr)$ |  | (50) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Comparing with the standard Fourier series $g(e^{i\theta})=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{m}e^{im\theta}$, we identify:

|  | $c_{0}=\frac{a_{0}}{2},\qquad c_{k}=\frac{a_{k}}{2},\qquad c_{-k}=\frac{a_{k}}{2}\quad\text{for }k\geq 1$ |  | (51) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

*Step 3: Cauchy Integral Formula and Coefficient Bounds.* By analyticity of $g$ in $&#124;w&#124;<\rho$, each $c_{m}$ can be written via Cauchy’s
integral formula on the circle $&#124;w&#124;=\rho$:$c_{m}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{&#124;w&#124;=\rho}\frac{g(w)}{w^{m+1}}\,dw,m\in\mathbb{Z}$. Taking absolute values and using [Equation 48](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E48 "Equation 48 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and $&#124;w&#124;=\rho$, we obtain:

|  | $&#124;c_{m}&#124;\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{&#124;w&#124;=\rho}\frac{&#124;g(w)&#124;}{&#124;w&#124;^{&#124;m&#124;+1}}\,&#124;dw&#124;\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{&#124;w&#124;=\rho}\frac{M}{\rho^{&#124;m&#124;+1}}\,&#124;dw&#124;=\frac{M}{2\pi\rho^{&#124;m&#124;+1}}\cdot 2\pi\rho=\frac{M}{\rho^{&#124;m&#124;}}$ |  | (52) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

In particular, for $k\geq 1$ we have $&#124;c_{k}&#124;\leq\frac{M}{\rho^{k}}\quad\text{and}\quad&#124;c_{-k}&#124;\leq\frac{M}{\rho^{k}}$. By [Equation 51](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E51 "Equation 51 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), $a_{k}=2c_{k}=2c_{-k}$ for $k\geq 1$, we obtain exactly [Equation 47](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E47 "Equation 47 ‣ Lemma 5 (Chebyshev Coefficients under Bernstein Analyticity). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") as:

|  | $&#124;a_{k}&#124;=2&#124;c_{k}&#124;\leq\frac{2M}{\rho^{k}}$ |  | (53) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

∎

We now use this coefficient bound to control the truncation error of the
Chebyshev series, and then lift this scalar bound to an operator bound for
matrix-valued spectral filters.

###### Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses).

Assume that the rescaled response$\tilde{f}(\xi):=f\big(\tfrac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}(\xi+1)\big)$ admits an analytic
extension to the Bernstein ellipse $E_{\rho}$ for some $\rho>1$, and let$M:=\max_{z\in E_{\rho}}&#124;\tilde{f}(z)&#124;$. Let $p^{(K)}$ be the degree-$K$ Chebyshev truncation of $\tilde{f}$ on $[-1,1]$. Then:

|  | $\sup_{\xi\in[-1,1]}\big&#124;\tilde{f}(\xi)-p^{(K)}(\xi)\big&#124;\;\leq\;\frac{2M}{\rho^{K}(\rho-1)}$ |  | (54) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Consequently, for any symmetric PSD $L$ with spectrum $\sigma(L)\subset[0,\lambda_{\max}]$ and $\widetilde{L}$ as in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"):

|  | $\big\&#124;f(L)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})\big\&#124;_{2}\;\leq\;\frac{2M}{\rho^{K}(\rho-1)}$ |  | (55) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

In particular, the heat kernel $f(\lambda)=e^{-t\lambda}$ admits exponentially
convergent Chebyshev approximations on $[0,\lambda_{\max}]$ for any fixed$t>0$.

###### Proof.

We split the proof into two parts: first we bound the scalar approximation
error of $\tilde{f}$ by its degree-$K$ Chebyshev truncation on $[-1,1]$, then we
transfer the bound to the operator setting via the spectral decomposition of$L$ and the affine rescaling $\lambda\leftrightarrow\xi$.

*Step 1: Scalar Chebyshev Truncation Error on $[-1,1]$.* Let $\tilde{f}(\xi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}T_{k}(\xi),\xi\in[-1,1]$ be the Chebyshev series expansion of $\tilde{f}$ on $[-1,1]$, which converges uniformly on $[-1,1]$ under our analyticity assumption. Denote by $p^{(K)}$ the degree-$K$ truncation $p^{(K)}(\xi):=\sum_{k=0}^{K}a_{k}T_{k}(\xi)$. The truncation error (the tail of the series) is then:

|  | $R_{K}(\xi):=\tilde{f}(\xi)-p^{(K)}(\xi)=\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}a_{k}T_{k}(\xi)$ |  | (56) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Using the uniform bound $&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;\leq 1$ for all $\xi\in[-1,1]$ and [Equation 47](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E47 "Equation 47 ‣ Lemma 5 (Chebyshev Coefficients under Bernstein Analyticity). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), we obtain:

|  | $&#124;R_{K}(\xi)&#124;\leq\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}&#124;a_{k}&#124;\,&#124;T_{k}(\xi)&#124;\leq\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}&#124;a_{k}&#124;\leq\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{2M}{\rho^{k}}\quad\text{for all }\xi\in[-1,1]$ |  | (57) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The remaining sum is geometric $\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\rho^{k}}=\frac{\rho^{-(K+1)}}{1-\rho^{-1}}=\frac{\rho^{-K}}{\rho(\,1-\rho^{-1}\,)}=\frac{\rho^{-K}}{\rho-1}$. Plugging this into [Equation 57](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E57 "Equation 57 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") yields:

|  | $&#124;R_{K}(\xi)&#124;\leq 2M\cdot\frac{\rho^{-K}}{\rho-1}=\frac{2M}{\rho^{K}(\rho-1)}\quad\text{for all }\xi\in[-1,1]$ |  | (58) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Taking the supremum over $\xi\in[-1,1]$ gives exactly [Equation 54](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E54 "Equation 54 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"):

|  | $\sup_{\xi\in[-1,1]}\big&#124;\tilde{f}(\xi)-p^{(K)}(\xi)\big&#124;=\sup_{\xi\in[-1,1]}&#124;R_{K}(\xi)&#124;\leq\frac{2M}{\rho^{K}(\rho-1)}$ |  | (59) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

*Step 2: Lifting the Scalar Bound to Operators.* Let $L$ be a real symmetric PSD matrix with eigenvalues$\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{nd}$ contained in $[0,\lambda_{\max}]$, and
spectral decomposition $L=U\Lambda U^{\top},\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{nd})$ with $U$ orthogonal. Define the affine rescaling as in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). By functional calculus, we have $f(L)=Uf(\Lambda)U^{\top},p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})=U\,p^{(K)}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\,U^{\top}$ where $f(\Lambda)$ is diagonal with entries $f(\lambda_{i})$, and $p^{(K)}(\widetilde{\Lambda})$ is diagonal with entries $p^{(K)}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})=p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))$. Hence, $f(L)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})=U\big(f(\Lambda)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\big)U^{\top}$. The spectral (operator) norm is invariant under orthogonal conjugation, so:

|  | $\big\&#124;f(L)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})\big\&#124;_{2}=\big\&#124;f(\Lambda)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\big\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (60) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The right-hand side is the operator norm of a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are $d_{i}:=f(\lambda_{i})-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))$. The spectral norm of a diagonal matrix is just the maximum absolute value of its diagonal entries, thus:

|  | $\big\&#124;f(\Lambda)-p^{(K)}(\widetilde{\Lambda})\big\&#124;_{2}=\max_{1\leq i\leq nd}&#124;d_{i}&#124;=\max_{\lambda_{i}\in\sigma(L)}\big&#124;f(\lambda_{i})-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))\big&#124;$ |  | (61) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

By definition of $\tilde{f}$, we have $\tilde{f}(\xi(\lambda))=f\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}(\xi(\lambda)+1)\Bigr)=f(\lambda)$, so $f(\lambda_{i})=\tilde{f}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))$. Therefore for each eigenvalue $\lambda_{i}$ we can rewrite $\big&#124;f(\lambda_{i})-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))\big&#124;=\big&#124;\tilde{f}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))\big&#124;$. Since $\xi(\lambda_{i})\in[-1,1]$, [Equation 54](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E54 "Equation 54 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") gives $\big&#124;\tilde{f}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))-p^{(K)}(\xi(\lambda_{i}))\big&#124;\leq\frac{2M}{\rho^{K}(\rho-1)}$. Taking the maximum over all $i$ and combining [Equation 60](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E60 "Equation 60 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")–[Equation 61](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E61 "Equation 61 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") yields precisely [Equation 55](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E55 "Equation 55 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

*Step 3: Application to Heat Kernels.* For the heat kernel $f(\lambda)=e^{-t\lambda}$ with any fixed $t>0$, the
rescaled function: $\tilde{f}(\xi)=f\Bigl(\tfrac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}(\xi+1)\Bigr)=\exp\!\Bigl(-t\,\tfrac{\lambda_{\max}}{2}(\xi+1)\Bigr)$ is an entire function of $\xi\in\mathbb{C}$, hence analytic on every Bernstein ellipse $E_{\rho}$ and bounded by some finite $M_{\rho}$. Thus the assumptions of the theorem hold for all $\rho>1$, and we conclude that the Chebyshev truncations $p^{(K)}$ approximate $f(L)$ with an error that decays
exponentially in $K$ as in [Equation 55](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E55 "Equation 55 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").
∎

[Theorem 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") formalises the intuition behind the
Chebyshev-PolyNSD design: once the Laplacian spectrum is rescaled to $[-1,1]$, the quality of the approximation $p^{(K)}(\widetilde{L})\approx f(L)$ is controlled by *how far* the target response $f$ extends analytically into the complex plane, as quantified by the largest Bernstein ellipse $E_{\rho}$ on which $\tilde{f}$ remains analytic and bounded. The larger this ellipse (i.e., the larger $\rho$), the faster the error decreases geometrically in the degree $K$ with factor $\rho^{-K}$. The theorem makes the *spectral sharpness* and *analyticity* trade-off explicit: sharper spectral filters require either a higher polynomial degree $K$ or a relaxation of the desired uniform accuracy, whereas smoother filters (such as diffusions) can be captured extremely well with modest $K$.

In addition to that, the scalar bound in [Equation 54](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E54 "Equation 54 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") directly controls the operator approximation error in [Equation 55](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E55 "Equation 55 ‣ Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") through the spectral calculus. Combined with the $K$-hop locality result for polynomial filters (see [subsection A.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A1.SS5 "A.5 Polynomial Filters: K-hop Locality and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")), this yields a clean picture: a single Chebyshev-PolyNSD layer with degree $K$ implements a *strictly $K$-hop
local* operator whose action approximates a global diffusion semigroup$e^{-tL}$ (or, more generally, any analytic response $f(L)$) up to an error
decaying like $\rho^{-K}$. PolyNSD trades off the depth of the
GNN with the polynomial degree $K$, while retaining both locality and a
spectral approximation guarantee.

Finally, the argument is completely agnostic to the particular choice of $f$:
any spectral response that is analytic on a Bernstein ellipse containing the
rescaled spectrum can be uniformly approximated by Chebyshev polynomials with
an explicit geometric rate. This covers not only diffusion semigroups but also
families of low-pass, band-pass, and high-pass filters used in graph signal
processing, provided they are regularised to admit an analytic extension on a
sufficiently large $E_{\rho}$. So, [Theorem 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Exponential Chebyshev Convergence for Analytic Spectral Responses). ‣ B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") gives us the approximation-theoretic backbone for Chebyshev-PolyNSD: it guarantees that PolyNSD layers can approximate a wide class of smooth spectral filters (in particular diffusion semigroups), with exponentially small error in the polynomial degree, while retaining strict $K$-hop locality and the stability properties established in [subsection A.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A1.SS6 "A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

### B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration

Chebyshev rescaling requires an upper spectral bound $\lambda_{\max}$ for the chosen Laplacian $L$, so that the affine map in [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), has spectrum in $[-1,1]$.
In PolyNSD we use two strategies to obtain such a bound:
(i) *closed-form spectral enclosures* for normalised sheaf Laplacians and
for unnormalised Laplacians via Gershgorin-type arguments, and
(ii) a *short power iteration* to refine (or replace) the analytic bound
on unnormalised operators. Both approaches are cheap for sparse sheaf
Laplacians and provide the structural precondition needed by [subsection B.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS3 "B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and [subsection A.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A1.SS6 "A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").

##### Normalised Sheaf Laplacian.

For the degree-normalised sheaf Laplacian$\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}=D^{-1/2}L_{\mathcal{F}}D^{-1/2}$, the situation is
directly analogous to the scalar graph case: all eigenvalues lie in $[0,2]$.

###### Proposition 7 (Spectral Enclosure for the Normalised Sheaf Laplacian).

Let $\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}$ be the degree-normalised (vertex) sheaf Laplacian on
a finite sheaf $\mathcal{F}$ over $G=(V,E)$. Then $\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}$ is
symmetric positive semidefinite and its spectrum satisfies$\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}})\subset[0,2]$.

###### Proof.

Symmetry and positive semidefiniteness follow from the usual sheaf Laplacian
construction: $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ is symmetric PSD as a discrete Hodge Laplacian
and $D^{-1/2}$ is symmetric and invertible, so$\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}=D^{-1/2}L_{\mathcal{F}}D^{-1/2}$ is symmetric and
PSD as well. For the upper bound, we use a Rayleigh quotient argument. For any nonzero$x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})$, the Rayleigh quotient of $\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}$ is:

|  | $R(x):=\frac{\langle x,\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}x\rangle}{\&#124;x\&#124;_{2}^{2}}=\frac{\langle D^{-1/2}x,\ L_{\mathcal{F}}D^{-1/2}x\rangle}{\&#124;x\&#124;_{2}^{2}}=\frac{\langle y,L_{\mathcal{F}}y\rangle}{\langle D^{1/2}y,D^{1/2}y\rangle}$ |  | (62) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

where the last equality holds if we have $y=D^{-1/2}x$. The numerator has the usual sheaf Dirichlet form representation $\langle y,L_{\mathcal{F}}y\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(u,v)\in E}\big\&#124;T_{vu}y_{u}-y_{v}\big\&#124;^{2}_{\mathcal{F}_{v}}\;\geq\;0$, while the denominator is a weighted norm$\langle D^{1/2}y,D^{1/2}y\rangle=\sum_{v}\langle D_{v}y_{v},y_{v}\rangle$.
The same computation as in the scalar normalised Laplacian case shows that $0\;\leq\;R(x)\;\leq\;2,\forall x\neq 0$ because the local contributions at each edge are controlled by the incident degrees. Since the eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix are exactly the extremal values of its Rayleigh quotient, this implies $\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}})\subset[0,2]$.
∎

In practice, for normalised sheaf Laplacians we simply set $\lambda_{\max}=2$,
which guarantees $\sigma(\widetilde{L})\subset[-1,1]$ without any numerical
estimation.

##### Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Analytic Bound via Gershgorin Discs.

When working with an unnormalised sheaf Laplacian$L=L_{\mathcal{F}}=D-A$, we can obtain a cheap but safe upper bound on the
spectral radius by combining Gershgorin’s theorem with the structural
constraints on $D$ and $A$.

###### Proposition 8 (Gershgorin-Type Bound for Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacians).

Let $L_{\mathcal{F}}=D-A$ be an unnormalised sheaf Laplacian, with$D=\mathrm{blkdiag}(D_{v})\succeq 0$ block-diagonal over vertices and$A$ supported on off-diagonal edge blocks (so that the diagonal of $A$ is
zero). Assume that for each vertex $v$ the diagonal block $D_{v}$ dominates
the outgoing sheaf couplings in the sense that:

|  | $\sum_{u\neq v}\&#124;A_{vu}\&#124;_{2}\;\leq\;\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (63) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Then $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ is symmetric PSD and its largest eigenvalue satisfies:

|  | $\lambda_{\max}(L_{\mathcal{F}})\;\leq\;2\max_{v\in V}\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (64) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

In the scalar trivial-sheaf case ($\dim\mathcal{F}_{v}=1$, $D_{v}=\deg(v)$) this
reduces to the familiar bound $\lambda_{\max}\leq 2\max_{v}\deg(v)$.

###### Proof.

Positive semidefiniteness follows from the usual sheaf Laplacian construction
(as for $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ above), so all eigenvalues are real and nonnegative. We now apply Gershgorin’s disc theorem to the full matrix $L_{\mathcal{F}}$, viewed as an $N\times N$ real symmetric matrix with $N=\sum_{v}\dim\mathcal{F}_{v}$. Let $L_{ij}$ denote its scalar entries, and let $\lambda$ be any eigenvalue. Gershgorin’s theorem guarantees that there exists a row index $i$ such that $&#124;L_{ii}-\lambda&#124;\;\leq\;\sum_{j\neq i}&#124;L_{ij}&#124;$. The row index $i$ belongs to some vertex fibre $\mathcal{F}_{v}$, so the diagonal entry $L_{ii}$ is one of the diagonal entries of the block $D_{v}$. Hence $&#124;L_{ii}&#124;\leq\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}$. On the other hand, the off-diagonal entries $L_{ij}$ in that row come from the off-diagonal sheaf blocks $\{-A_{vu}\}_{u\neq v}$, so by the triangle inequality and the definition of the operator norm, we will have $\sum_{j\neq i}&#124;L_{ij}&#124;\;\leq\;\sum_{u\neq v}\&#124;A_{vu}\&#124;_{2}\;\leq\;\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}$ by the domination assumption [Equation 63](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E63 "Equation 63 ‣ Proposition 8 (Gershgorin-Type Bound for Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacians). ‣ Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Analytic Bound via Gershgorin Discs. ‣ B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). Therefore, the corresponding Gershgorin disc for row $i$ is contained in the real interval:

|  | $[L_{ii}-R_{i},\ L_{ii}+R_{i}]\subseteq[-\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2},\ 2\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}],\qquad R_{i}:=\sum_{j\neq i}&#124;L_{ij}&#124;$ |  | (65) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Since $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ is symmetric, all its eigenvalues are real and must lie
in the union of these intervals over all rows $i$, and hence over all vertices$v$. Intersecting with $[0,\infty)$ (because $L_{\mathcal{F}}$ is PSD), we obtain $0\;\leq\;\lambda\;\leq\;2\max_{v\in V}\&#124;D_{v}\&#124;_{2}$ for every eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $L_{\mathcal{F}}$, which proves the claim.
In the scalar case, $D_{v}=\deg(v)$ and the off-diagonal entries in row $v$ are $-1$ along each incident edge, so $R_{i}=\deg(v)$ and the usual scalar
Gershgorin discs $[0,2\deg(v)]$ are recovered.
∎

In PolyNSD, the Gershgorin-type bound of [Proposition 8](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition8 "Proposition 8 (Gershgorin-Type Bound for Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacians). ‣ Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Analytic Bound via Gershgorin Discs. ‣ B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") provides a very fast, purely local estimate
of $\lambda_{\max}$ that is guaranteed to be safe for Chebyshev rescaling,
even though it can be somewhat conservative.

##### Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Power Iteration.

To obtain a tighter estimate of the largest eigenvalue of $L_{\mathcal{F}}$, we can refine (or replace) the analytic bound via a short power iteration. For a symmetric matrix $L$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0$,
the classical power method repeatedly applies $L$ and normalises: $x^{(t+1)}\;\leftarrow\;\frac{Lx^{(t)}}{\&#124;Lx^{(t)}\&#124;_{2}},\&#124;x^{(0)}\&#124;_{2}=1$, and uses the Rayleigh quotient $\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}:=\frac{\langle x^{(t)},Lx^{(t)}\rangle}{\&#124;x^{(t)}\&#124;_{2}^{2}}=\langle x^{(t)},Lx^{(t)}\rangle$ as an approximation to $\lambda_{1}$. The next proposition states a standard error bound.

###### Proposition 9 (Power-Iteration Error).

Let $L=U\Lambda U^{\top}$ be real symmetric with eigenvalues$\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0$ and orthonormal eigenvectors$\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$. Let $x^{(0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}u_{i}$ with $\alpha_{1}\neq 0$ and define $x^{(t)}$ and $\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}$ as above. Set$r:=\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}\in(0,1)$. Then for every $t\geq 0$:

|  | $0\;\leq\;\lambda_{1}-\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}\;\leq\;(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\,r^{2t}\,\frac{\&#124;x^{(0)}-\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle u_{1}\&#124;_{2}^{2}}{\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle^{2}}$ |  | (66) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

In particular, the Rayleigh quotient converges to $\lambda_{1}$ at a geometric
rate $r^{2t}$ as $t$ increases.

###### Proof.

We make the dependence on the spectral decomposition explicit. Since$L=U\Lambda U^{\top}$ and the eigenvectors form an orthonormal basis, we can
write: $x^{(0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}u_{i},\alpha_{i}=\langle^{(0)},u_{i}\rangle$. After $t$ steps of the (unnormalised) power method, we have $L^{t}x^{(0)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}^{t}u_{i}$. Normalising, then yields:

|  | $x^{(t)}=\frac{L^{t}x^{(0)}}{\&#124;L^{t}x^{(0)}\&#124;_{2}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}^{t}u_{i}}{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}\Big)^{1/2}}$ |  | (67) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

so the coordinates of $x^{(t)}$ in the eigenbasis are: $\beta_{i}^{(t)}:=\langle x^{(t)},u_{i}\rangle=\frac{\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}^{t}}{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2t}\Big)^{1/2}}$. The Rayleigh quotient at step $t$ can then be expressed as:

|  | $\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}=\langle x^{(t)},Lx^{(t)}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\big(\beta_{i}^{(t)}\big)^{2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t+1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}}$ |  | (68) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

*Nonnegativity of the Error.* We first show that $\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}\leq\lambda_{1}$. Since$\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{i}$ for all $i$, we have $\lambda_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}\;\geq\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t+1}$ and hence:

|  | $\lambda_{1}-\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}=\frac{\lambda_{1}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t+1}}{\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}}\;\geq\;0$ |  | (69) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Thus $0\leq\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}\leq\lambda_{1}$ for all $t$.

*Closed-Form Expression for the Error.* We can rewrite the numerator more transparently by factoring $\lambda_{1}$:

|  | $\lambda_{1}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i})\lambda_{i}^{2t}$ |  | (70) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The $i=1$ term vanishes because $\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}=0$, so: $\lambda_{1}-\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}=\frac{\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i})\lambda_{i}^{2t}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}}$.

*Upper Bound via the Spectral Gap.* For $i\geq 2$ we have $\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{2}$ and$\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}$. Therefore:

|  | $\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i})\lambda_{i}^{2t}\;\leq\;(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}\;\leq\;(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\lambda_{2}^{2t}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}$ |  | (71) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

On the other hand, the denominator is bounded from below by the $i=1$ term $\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2t}\;\geq\;\alpha_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2t}$. Combining these two inequalities yields:

|  | $\lambda_{1}-\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}\;\leq\;(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\,\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2t}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}^{2t}}=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\,\Big(\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\Big)^{2t}\,\frac{\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}$ |  | (72) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Recalling that $r=\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}\in(0,1)$, we identify:

|  | $\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}=\&#124;x^{(0)}-\alpha_{1}u_{1}\&#124;_{2}^{2}=\&#124;x^{(0)}-\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle u_{1}\&#124;_{2}^{2},\qquad\alpha_{1}=\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle$ |  | (73) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

so that we have the following equation that we can substitute in the previous inequality:

|  | $\frac{\sum_{i=2}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}=\frac{\&#124;x^{(0)}-\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle u_{1}\&#124;_{2}^{2}}{\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle^{2}},$ |  | (74) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

|  | $\lambda_{1}-\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}\;\leq\;(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\,r^{2t}\,\frac{\&#124;x^{(0)}-\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle u_{1}\&#124;_{2}^{2}}{\langle x^{(0)},u_{1}\rangle^{2}}$ |  | (75) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Since $0<r<1$, the prefactor $r^{2t}$ ensures geometric
convergence of $\widehat{\lambda}^{(t)}$ to $\lambda_{1}$.
∎

In PolyNSD we typically combine these ingredients as follows. For normalised
sheaf Laplacians we fix $\lambda_{\max}=2$ by [Proposition 7](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition7 "Proposition 7 (Spectral Enclosure for the Normalised Sheaf Laplacian). ‣ Normalised Sheaf Laplacian. ‣ B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). For unnormalised sheaf Laplacians we
initialise $\lambda_{\max}$ using the Gershgorin-type analytic bound of [Proposition 8](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition8 "Proposition 8 (Gershgorin-Type Bound for Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacians). ‣ Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Analytic Bound via Gershgorin Discs. ‣ B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), which requires only local degree information,
and (optionally) refine it with $5$–$10$ steps of power iteration as in [Proposition 9](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition9 "Proposition 9 (Power-Iteration Error). ‣ Unnormalised Sheaf Laplacian: Power Iteration. ‣ B.4 Estimating the Spectral Scale: Analytic Bound and Power Iteration ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). This yields a spectrally safe rescaling$\widetilde{L}$ for the Chebyshev-PolyNSD layer, while keeping the overhead
negligible compared to the overall training cost.

### B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating

We conclude the analysis of PolyNSD by formalising the spectral effect and
stability properties of the *high-pass correction* and *gated
residual* used in the main text, and by explaining why they are structurally
useful in the design of our PolyNSD layers. A single PolyNSD layer applies, before the pointwise nonlinearity $\phi$, the linear transformation:

|  | $\displaystyle h_{\mathrm{hp}}$ | $\displaystyle:=x-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}Lx$ |  | (76) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |
|  | $\displaystyle z$ | $\displaystyle:=p(\widetilde{L})x+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\,h_{\mathrm{hp}}$ |  | (77) |
|  | $\displaystyle x^{+}$ | $\displaystyle:=(I+\tanh\varepsilon)x-\phi(z)$ |  | (78) |

where $\widetilde{L}$ is defined as [Equation 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 3 Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and it is the spectrally rescaled Laplacian, $p$ is a polynomial filter (typically a convex Chebyshev mixture as in [subsection A.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A1.SS6 "A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")), $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\in\mathbb{R}$ is a learnable scalar, $\varepsilon$ is a learnable diagonal parameter (so $I+\tanh\varepsilon$ is a diagonal residual gate), and $\phi$ is a component-wise nonlinearity with Lipschitz constant $\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\leq 1$. The term $p(\widetilde{L})x$ plays the role of a learned, spectrally controlled diffusion-like filter, whose response is bounded and well approximated as in [subsection B.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS3 "B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). The additional term $h_{\mathrm{hp}}=x-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}Lx$ is a simple linear spectral correction: it is affine in $L$, cheap to compute, and has a closed-form frequency response that can be combined with $p$ to compensate for excessive low-pass bias. The residual gate $(I+\tanh\varepsilon)$ then allows us to tune the deviation from the identity map while keeping a global Lipschitz control.

###### Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound).

Let $L=U\Lambda U^{\top}$ be symmetric positive semidefinite, with eigenvalues$\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{nd}$ and orthonormal eigenvectors $\{u_{i}\}$, and let$\widetilde{L}=\tfrac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}L-I$ with$\sigma(L)\subset[0,\lambda_{\max}]$. Let $p$ be any real polynomial such that$\&#124;p(\widetilde{L})\&#124;_{2}\leq 1$.
Then:

1. 1\.

   In the eigenbasis of $L$, the linear operator $x\mapsto p(\widetilde{L})x+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}h_{\mathrm{hp}}$ has per-eigenvalue multiplier:

    |  | $m(\lambda)=p\!\Big(\tfrac{2\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}-1\Big)+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\Big(1-\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}\Big),\qquad\lambda\in\sigma(L)$ |  | (79) |
    | --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

   In particular, if $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}>0$ and $p(\xi)\geq 0$ for all $\xi\in[-1,1]$, then $m(\lambda)>0$ for all $\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{\max})$, so no non-harmonic mode
   ($\lambda>0$ with $\lambda<\lambda_{\max}$) can be annihilated.

2. 2\.

   The full mapping $T:x\mapsto x^{+}$ satisfies the global Lipschitz bound:

    |  | $\&#124;T(x)-T(y)\&#124;_{2}\;\leq\;\Bigl[\bigl(1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}\bigr)+\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,\bigl(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\bigr)\Bigr]\,\&#124;x-y\&#124;_{2},$ |  | (80) |
    | --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

   i.e. $T$ is Lipschitz with constant at most: $L_{T}\;\leq\;\bigl(1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}\bigr)+\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,\bigl(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\bigr)$.

###### Proof.

We treat the two claims separately.

*(1) Spectral Multiplier of the High-pass Skip.*

Since $\widetilde{L}$ is an affine function of $L$, we have $L\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{L}L$ and the two operators are
simultaneously diagonalisable by the same orthogonal basis $U$. Writing $L=U\Lambda U^{\top},\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{nd})$, we obtain:

|  | $\widetilde{L}=\tfrac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}U\Lambda U^{\top}-I=U\Big(\tfrac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}\Lambda-I\Big)U^{\top}=U\widetilde{\Lambda}U^{\top}$ |  | (81) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

with $\widetilde{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})$ and $\tilde{\lambda}_{i}=\tfrac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}\lambda_{i}-1\;\in\;[-1,1]\text{for all }i$,
by $\sigma(L)\subset[0,\lambda_{\max}]$. Let $x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})$ and write $x=U\hat{x}$ with $\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{i})_{i}$. By polynomial functional calculus, we have: $p(\widetilde{L})x=U\,p(\widetilde{\Lambda})\hat{x}=U\big(\mathrm{diag}(p(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}))\hat{x}\big)$ so the $i$-th Fourier coefficient of $p(\widetilde{L})x$ is:$p(\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\hat{x}_{i}=p(\tfrac{2\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{\max}}-1)\,\hat{x}_{i}$. Similarly, for the
high-pass term we have:

|  | $h_{\mathrm{hp}}=\Big(I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L\Big)x=U\Big(I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}\Lambda\Big)\hat{x}$ |  | (82) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

so its $i$-th Fourier coefficient is then $\Big(1-\tfrac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{\max}}\Big)\hat{x}_{i}$. Putting these together, the pre-nonlinearity linear combination $x\mapsto p(\widetilde{L})x+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}h_{\mathrm{hp}}$ acts diagonally in the eigenbasis as:

|  | $\hat{x}_{i}\;\longmapsto\;\Big[p\!\Big(\tfrac{2\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{\max}}-1\Big)+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\Big(1-\tfrac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{\max}}\Big)\Big]\hat{x}_{i}$ |  | (83) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

which is exactly the multiplier $m(\lambda_{i})$ in [Equation 79](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E79 "Equation 79 ‣ Item 1 ‣ Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"). For the positivity statement, assume$\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}>0$ and $p(\xi)\geq 0$ on $[-1,1]$. For any eigenvalue$\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{\max})$ we have $1-\lambda/\lambda_{\max}>0$, hence, $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\Big(1-\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}\Big)>0$ and the additional term $p(\tfrac{2\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}-1)\geq 0$ by hypothesis. Therefore $m(\lambda)>0$ for all $\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{\max})$, so no non-harmonic
eigenmode can be annihilated. The only potential zero of $m$ on the spectrum
can appear at $\lambda=\lambda_{\max}$, e.g. if $p(1)=0$ and$\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}=0$. *(2) Global Lipschitz Bound for the Full Update.*

Let $x,y$ be two arbitrary inputs and set: $\Delta x:=x-y,\Delta z:=z(x)-z(y)$. Then, $T(x)-T(y)=(I+\tanh\varepsilon)\Delta x-\bigl(\phi\big(z(x)\big)-\phi\big(z(y)\big)\bigr)$ So by the triangle inequality, we have:

|  | $\&#124;T(x)-T(y)\&#124;_{2}\leq\&#124;(I+\tanh\varepsilon)\Delta x\&#124;_{2}+\&#124;\phi(z(x))-\phi(z(y))\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (84) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

We first bound the residual gate term. Since $I+\tanh\varepsilon$ is diagonal, its spectral norm equals the maximum absolute value of its diagonal entries:

|  | $\&#124;I+\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{2}=\max_{j}&#124;1+\tanh\varepsilon_{j}&#124;\leq 1+\max_{j}&#124;\tanh\varepsilon_{j}&#124;=1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}$ |  | (85) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Hence,

|  | $\&#124;(I+\tanh\varepsilon)\Delta x\&#124;_{2}\leq\bigl(1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}\bigr)\,\&#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (86) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Next we control the nonlinear term. By Lipschitzness of $\phi$ with constant$\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)$, we have:

|  | $\&#124;\phi(z(x))-\phi(z(y))\&#124;_{2}\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,\&#124;\Delta z\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (87) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

The increment $\Delta z$ can then be written as $\Delta z=p(\widetilde{L})\Delta x+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\Big(I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L\Big)\Delta x$, so by the triangle inequality and submultiplicativity of the operator norm:

|  | $\&#124;\Delta z\&#124;_{2}\leq\&#124;p(\widetilde{L})\&#124;_{2}\,\&#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}+&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\,\Big\&#124;I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L\Big\&#124;_{2}\,\&#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (88) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

By hypothesis $\&#124;p(\widetilde{L})\&#124;_{2}\leq 1$. Moreover, since $L$ is symmetric
PSD with eigenvalues $\lambda_{i}\in[0,\lambda_{\max}]$, the eigenvalues of$I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L$ are $\mu_{i}=1-\lambda_{i}/\lambda_{\max}\in[0,1]$,
so in fact $\big\&#124;I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L\big\&#124;_{2}\leq 1$. Using instead
the looser bound $\big\&#124;I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L\big\&#124;_{2}\leq 2$ (which
remains valid even if $\lambda_{\max}$ is only an upper bound on the spectrum), we obtain from [Equation 88](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E88 "Equation 88 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"):

|  | $\&#124;\Delta z\&#124;_{2}\leq\bigl(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\bigr)\,\\ &#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (89) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

∎

###### Proof.

Combining [Equation 87](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E87 "Equation 87 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and [Equation 89](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E89 "Equation 89 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") yields:

|  | $\&#124;\phi(z(x))-\phi(z(y))\&#124;_{2}\leq\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,\bigl(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\bigr)\,\&#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (90) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

Substituting together with [Equation 86](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E86 "Equation 86 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") into [Equation 84](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E84 "Equation 84 ‣ Proof. ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), we obtain then:

|  | $\&#124;T(x)-T(y)\&#124;_{2}\leq\Bigl[\bigl(1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}\bigr)+\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,\bigl(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;\bigr)\Bigr]\&#124;\Delta x\&#124;_{2}$ |  | (91) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

which is exactly the claimed Lipschitz bound: [Equation 80](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E80 "Equation 80 ‣ Item 2 ‣ Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves").
∎

##### High-pass Skip and Residual Gating.

[Equation 79](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E79 "Equation 79 ‣ Item 1 ‣ Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") shows that the frequency response $m(\lambda)$ of the linear core is a *sum of two simple terms*: a learned polynomial$p(\tfrac{2\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}-1)$ and a linear profile$\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}(1-\lambda/\lambda_{\max})$. If $p$ is chosen to
approximate a diffusion semigroup $e^{-t\lambda}$ (or any analytic low-pass
filter as in [subsection B.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS3 "B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves")), then $p$ is typically strongly
decreasing in $\lambda$, i.e. $p(0)\approx 1$ and $p(\lambda_{\max})\approx 0$.
In isolation, this tends to *aggressively suppress* high-frequency modes, and repeated application of such a filter can readily lead to
oversmoothing. The correction $\lambda\;\longmapsto\;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}\Big(1-\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\max}}\Big)$ is a simple knob that deforms this pure diffusion profile. For instance, indeed:

- •

  If $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}<0$ with small magnitude, then $m(\lambda)=p(\cdot)+\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}(1-\lambda/\lambda_{\max})$ *subtracts more* from low frequencies (where $1-\lambda/\lambda_{\max}\approx 1$) than from high ones (where $1-\lambda/\lambda_{\max}\approx 0$), relatively flattening the low-pass behaviour and partially reweighting intermediate and high modes.

- •

  Conversely, if $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}>0$, the same term can be used to ensure $m(\lambda)$ remains strictly positive on $[0,\lambda_{\max})$ when $p\geq 0$, as captured in item (1) of [Proposition 10](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition10 "Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), so that no non-harmonic mode is accidentally annihilated by the learned polynomial.

In both cases, the effect is controlled by a *single scalar* and a linear function of the spectrum, making the impact on $m(\lambda)$ easy to analyse and to regularise. Second, the Lipschitz estimate in [Equation 80](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E80 "Equation 80 ‣ Item 2 ‣ Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") quantifies the stability cost of inserting both the high-pass correction and the gated residual. The term $1+\&#124;\tanh\varepsilon\&#124;_{\infty}$ bounds how far the residual branch deviates from the identity: when $\varepsilon$ is small, we have $\tanh\varepsilon\approx 0$ and the residual behaves nearly isometrically. The contribution $\mathrm{Lip}(\phi)\,(1+2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;)$ comes from the composition of a 1-Lipschitz nonlinearity with the pre-activation linear map $z(x)$: here $1$ corresponds to the nonexpansive polynomial $p(\widetilde{L})$ (by design of the Chebyshev-PolyNSD core), while $2&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;$ bounds the additional amplification introduced by the high-pass term $I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L$. This makes transparent the trade-off: larger $&#124;\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}&#124;$ allows more aggressive reshaping of the spectrum but increases the worst-case Lipschitz constant linearly and explicitly, which can be controlled during optimisation (e.g. via weight regularisation or explicit constraints on $\alpha_{\mathrm{hp}}$ and$\varepsilon$). Together with the approximation results of [subsection B.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.SS3 "B.3 Chebyshev Approximation of Diffusion Kernels ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") and the nonexpansive properties of Chebyshev
mixtures in [subsection A.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A1.SS6 "A.6 Chebyshev Filters: Spectral Rescaling, Norm Control and Energy Monotonicity ‣ Appendix A Spectral Theory applied to Sheaf Laplacians ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves"), [Proposition 10](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#Thmproposition10 "Proposition 10 (Spectral Shape and Global Lipschitz Bound). ‣ B.5 High-pass Skip and Residual Gating ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") therefore explains why PolyNSD augments
its diffusion-like polynomial core with exactly (i) a linear spectral
correction of the form $I-\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\max}}L$ and (ii) a diagonal
residual gate: they provide a low-complexity, analytically tractable way to
combat oversmoothing and to preserve informative high-frequency content,
while keeping the overall layer within a globally controlled Lipschitz
envelope.

### B.6 PolyNSD vs Neural Sheaf Diffusion: Operator Class

In this subsection we make precise the claim that Chebyshev-PolyNSD with
degree $K{=}1$ recovers the same diffusion operator class as Neural Sheaf
Diffusion (NSD) [[7](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#bib.bib11)], while for $K{>}1$ it strictly
generalises it to higher-order polynomials in the sheaf Laplacian. In order to do that, we focus on the *diffusion core*, i.e. the linear operator that acts on sheaf
0-cochains between pointwise nonlinearities and feature-mixing MLPs.

##### Canonical NSD Diffusion Core.

In Neural Sheaf Diffusion, once a sheaf Laplacian $L$ has been assembled
from learned restriction maps, the diffusion step acting on a 0-cochain$x\in C^{0}(\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F})$ can be written abstractly as:

|  | $x^{+}=Ax-BLx,$ |  | (92) |
| --- | ------------------------------ | --- | ---- |

where $A$ and $B$ are feature-wise scaling operators, encoding the step size and residual weighting. For the purposes of spectral
analysis, the key point is that, *for fixed $L$*, NSD’s diffusion core
always lies in the two-dimensional linear span $\{I,L\}$: it is a first-order
polynomial $aI+bL$, with $a,b$ determined by the learnable scalings in [Equation 92](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.00242v3#A2.E92 "Equation 92 ‣ Canonical NSD Diffusion Core. ‣ B.6 PolyNSD vs Neural Sheaf Diffusion: Operator Class ‣ Appendix B PolyNSD Layer ‣ Polynomial Neural Sheaf Diffusion: A Spectral Filtering Approach on Cellular Sheaves") (possibly different per feature channel).
